The fix is in the **import pipeline** (source data level), not in a reader-time hack. There are two approaches:

**Approach A (Recommended): Reorder senses in corpus-LITE.json**
- Change `"shortGloss": "the other of two; another; different"` to `"shortGloss": "another; the other of two; different"`
- Then re-import corpus: `POST /admin/import-corpus?reset=true` → this triggers `CorpusImportService.rebuildLexemeSenses()` which splits on `;` and creates `lexeme_sense` rows indexed 0, 1, 2
- Then regenerate: `POST /admin/regenerate-lite-glosses` to rebuild gloss_set_entry

**Approach B: SQL migration to reorder lexeme_sense**
- New Flyway migration that swaps sense_index 0 and 1 for G2087's lexeme_id
- Then regenerate glosses

Approach A is more fundamental — it fixes the source data. But it changes the gloss for *every* occurrence of G2087, not just 1 Cor 6:1. The question is: should "another" be the default sense for G2087? Looking at the senses:
- "the other of two" — this is the classical meaning (one of two pairs)
- "another" — this is more common in NT usage (another person/thing, indefinite)
- "different" — distinct from

Actually, ἕτερος in the NT is commonly used to mean "another" (indefinite, not the second of two). So making "another" sense 0 might be appropriate globally.

## [assistant] (21:38:45)

The fix goes in the **source data**, not in a reader-time table. Here's the pipeline:

### Where the gloss comes from

```
corpus-LITE.json: "shortGloss": "the other of two; another; different"
                       ↓ (CorpusImportService.rebuildLexemeSenses() splits on ";")
lexeme_sense: sense_index=0 → "the other of two"  ← this is what wins
              sense_index=1 → "another"
              sense_index=2 → "different"
                       ↓ (InflectionEngineService.fetchAllTokens() COALESCE)
gloss_set_entry: "the other of two"  ← what user sees
```

The `COALESCE` in the build pipeline always picks `lexeme_sense` where `sense_index = 0` as the default. For polysemous tokens, Handler C (`token_sense_override`) can pick a different index, but G2087 has `is_polysemous=false`.

### Two approaches to fix

**A — Reorder source data** (change `corpus-LITE.json`):
```
"shortGloss": "another; the other of two; different"
```
Then: `POST /admin/import-corpus?reset=true` → `POST /admin/regenerate-lite-glosses`
This changes sense 0 globally for G2087 (every verse).

**B — Lexeme-level data fix** (new Flyway migration):
Swap `sense_index` values in `lexeme_sense` for G2087's lexeme, keeping corpus-LITE.json unchanged. Then regenerate glosses.

Approach A is cleaner — it fixes the root. `ἕτερος` in NT usage more often means "another" (indefinite) than "the other of two" (dual). But it's a broader change. Which would you prefer?

The `sense_selection_rule` table (V46-V47) was a per-verse exception table. It mapped specific (strongs_id, book_id, chapter, verse_num) → selected_sense_index. 

The reasons it was a hack:

1. **Per-verse granularity, not semantic**: It doesn't fix the actual issue (the corpus sense ordering is wrong). It papered over the symptom for one specific verse. The same word in a different verse with the same meaning would still show the wrong gloss.

2. **Maintenance burden**: Every time a wrong gloss is found, you'd need to add another row to this table. It's an infinite treadmill of per-verse patches instead of fixing the source data once.

3. **Ignores the actual pipeline**: The gloss pipeline has a clear design — Handler C for sense disambiguation (AI, polysemous only), Handler D for build-time gloss resolution. A per-verse exception table bypasses both and just hardcodes answers.

4. **Data, not infrastructure**: The V48 comment says it best: "Per-verse exception rules proved to be the wrong approach — they are data hacks, not infrastructure." The table is just a list of known-bad glosses with manual corrections. That's not a system — it's a patch list.

5. **Doesn't scale**: For 138K tokens, even 1% wrong glosses = 1,380 entries. That's what `lexeme_sense` ordering is supposed to handle structurally.

6. **Wrong layer**: The fix was applied at build-time in `InflectionEngineService` (checking the table during regeneration) AND was attempted at read-time in `GlossChainService`. Both are wrong — the issue is in the source data (corpus-LITE.json → lexeme_sense ordering).

The previous AI kept trying to solve it by adding more infrastructure (a new table, a new service, wiring it into two different places) instead of just fixing the data. That's the classic "adding complexity to avoid changing a config file" anti-pattern.

## [assistant] (21:58:37)

You've nailed it. The gap is exactly there. Here's the current reality:

### How it works now (wrong)

| Step | What | Who gets it |
|------|------|-------------|
| Corpus import | `shortGloss` split on `;` → `lexeme_sense` with `sense_index=0,1,2...` | All 5,624 lexemes |
| Build pipeline | `COALESCE(ls_ai, ls[0], corpus)` — picks sense 0 unless `token_sense_override` exists | 2,339 tokens (13 polysemous words) get AI-selected sense |
| Everything else (136K tokens) | Falls through to **sense 0 always** | Every non-polysemous word |
| Reader time | `contextual_gloss` layer 2.5 (AI, 13 words), or falls to cache | Slow, gated to 13 words |

The build pipeline's `COALESCE` already supports per-token sense selection via `token_sense_override`:
```sql
COALESCE(ls_ai.gloss,         -- sense from token_sense_override.sense_index
         ls.gloss,             -- sense_index = 0 (default)
         m.short_gloss)        -- raw corpus fallback
```

But it only fires for **2,339 tokens** where `token_sense_override` has a row — the 13 polysemous words run through Handler C AI batch. The remaining ~136K tokens always get sense 0.

### The fix: sense disambiguation for ALL tokens at build time

The table already exists: `token_sense_override (verse_word_id UNIQUE, sense_index)`. We'd:

1. **Run AI batch (Groq)** for all 138K tokens — send each `(verse_word_id, verse_context, lexeme_id, sense_options)` and ask it to pick the winning `sense_index`
2. **Write to `token_sense_override`** — table already supports it, and the build pipeline `COALESCE` already reads it
3. **Regenerate glosses** — `regenerate-lite-glosses` picks up the new `ls_ai.gloss` instead of falling to sense 0

The read path stays fast because `gloss_set_entry` is precomputed — no AI calls at reader time.

### Storage

`token_sense_override` is the right table. Two options for populating:

| Approach | Cost | Detail |
|----------|------|--------|
| **Groq batch per token** | ~138K calls at 30 req/min = ~3 days | Send verse context, lexeme, sense list → AI picks sense_index |
| **Chunk by unique (strongs_id, morph_key)** | ~25K calls = ~14 hours | Group by same word + morphology, provide representative verses |

That's the gap. Want me to explore any of this further?

Let me address their questions first:

**Chunk by unique (strongs_id, morph_key):**
The idea is: instead of calling AI once per token (138K calls), group tokens by (strongs_id, morph_key). For example, G3056 (λόγος) with morph_key "verb_aorist_active_indicative_3_sg" might appear in 50 verses. You'd send one AI call with a representative verse, and apply the selected sense_index to all 50 tokens at once. This reduces calls from 138K to ~25K.

But this has a flaw: the same word with the same morphology can mean different things in different verses (λόγος in John 1:1 = "Word" vs Matt 5:37 = "statement"). So grouping by (strongs_id, morph_key) alone isn't sufficient — you need verse context.

A better chunking would be:
- For the 5,124 non-polysemous lexemes (single sense each, senseless to AI-disambiguate), just mark them as sense 0
- For ~500 truly ambiguous lexemes, run per-token AI
- Or: run per-token for all but use Groq batch with a fast model

**Round-robin free APIs:**
The user mentioned adding other free AI providers as a round robin. This would help with rate limiting. Groq free tier is ~30 req/min. If we add Together AI, GitHub Models, etc., we could parallelize.

**Python script → JSONL → admin endpoint:**
The user's idea: write a Python script that does the AI calls (using multiple providers in round-robin), writes results to a JSONL file, then an admin endpoint imports it into `token_sense_override`. This keeps AI work out of the server (which shouldn't be blocked by batch jobs) and follows the existing pattern (like ContextualGlossService's stage→review→commit).

This is actually the right pattern — the existing corpus synthesis does this (Python script → JSON file → admin import). And the contextual_gloss pipeline also stages to JSON before committing.

## [assistant] (22:09:07)

You're right — this deserves a proper story. Let me address your questions first, then we can shape the issue.

### The chunking idea

Grouping by `(strongs_id, morph_key)` would reduce 138K → ~25K AI calls, but it's **flawed** — same word + same morphology can mean different things in different contexts (John 1:1 λόγος vs Matt 5:37 λόγος). So you'd need per-token context anyway for ambiguous words.

Better split:
- **~5,124 lexemes with single sense** (e.g., G1722 ἐν) — no AI needed, always sense 0
- **~500 lexemes with 2+ senses** — these are the ones needing per-token disambiguation

That's maybe ~20K-50K truly ambiguous tokens, not 138K.

### Round-robin free APIs + Python JSONL approach

This is the cleanest pattern and it already exists in the codebase:

```
Python script (Groq + Together + GitHub Models round-robin)
    → ai_staging/sense_selection_{batch}.jsonl
    → POST /admin/import-sense-selections (new admin endpoint)
    → token_sense_override table
    → POST /admin/regenerate-lite-glosses
    → gloss_set_entry rebuilt with correct senses
```

This matches:
- **Corpus synthesis**: `synthesize_corpus.py → corpus-LITE.json → import-corpus`
- **Contextual gloss**: `populateChapter() → ai_staging/*.json → commitStaging()`

Keeps AI work off the server, produces an auditable JSONL file, and the commit is a single DB upsert.

For the multi-sense token query, I need to find all tokens where their lexeme has more than 1 sense. Let me think about the SQL:

```sql
SELECT vw.*, l.strongs_id, l.lemma
FROM verse_word vw
JOIN lexeme l ON vw.lexeme_id = l.id
WHERE (
    SELECT COUNT(*) FROM lexeme_sense ls 
    WHERE ls.lexeme_id = l.id
) > 1
```

This will give us all multi-sense tokens. Then for each unique strongs_id, fetch the lexicon definitions.

## [assistant] (23:01:44)

## Goal
- Build a build-time per-token sense disambiguation pipeline that selects the correct corpus sense_index for every multi-sense NT token using AI, replacing the current sense-0 fallback.

## Constraints & Preferences
- Two-mode workflow: collaborating (discussion, no code) vs execution (explicit "go ahead")
- Pipeline must use AI at build time (not read time) — zero latency impact on reader, uses precomputed gloss_set_entry
- AI is a SELECTOR only (picks from existing sense_index options, does not generate new text)
- Python script first for prototyping, then port to Java admin endpoint
- Staging to JSONL (ai_staging/sense_selection_*.jsonl) for audit trail before commit
- Round-robin free AI providers: Groq + Gemini + optionally GitHub Models / NVIDIA / Together
- Output must be grounded in curated lexeme_sense data — no hallucinated glosses
- Include full lexicon definitions (LSJ, Thayer, UGL, Abbott-Smith, Strong's) in the prompt as context
- Users's GITHUB_TOKEN is available in .env

## Progress
### Done
- Cleaned up stale sense_selection_rule changes (reverted InflectionEngineService.java to HEAD, deleted untracked V46-V48 migration files)
- Created GitHub issue #142: "feat: build-time per-token sense disambiguation for all lexemes (replace sense-0 fallback)" with full design doc
- Closed #139 as superseded by #142, linked #140 as resolved-by #142
- Wrote scripts/sense_disambiguate.py (full batch runner with --dry-run, --submit, --resume, --strongs, --limit flags, round-robin provider chain with rate-limit handling, lexicon definitions in prompt)
- Wrote scripts/test_passage.py (standalone test runner for single passage with --book, --chapter, --verses, --dry-run flags)
- Confirmed prompt works: dry-run shows rich lexicon context (UGL, LSJ, Thayer, Abbott-Smith, Strong's) alongside corpus senses
- Live test on 3 G2087 tokens (Matt 6:24, Matt 8:21) — all 3 correct via Groq (sense 0 for dual context, sense 1 for indefinite)

### In Progress
- Running test on 1 Cor 1:1-5 passage (dry-run was completed, live test output was shown but truncated)

### Blocked
- None

## Key Decisions
- Sense disambiguation happens at build time (not read time) — precomputed into gloss_set_entry via regenerate-lite-glosses
- AI selects sense_index only (SELECTOR, not WRITER) — prevents hallucination, output grounded in lexeme_sense data
- Prompt includes full lexicon definitions (LSJ/Thayer/UGL etc.) as context, not just the 3-word corpus sense label — this was the flaw in the old sense_selection_rule table and the original Handler C approach
- Python first for fast iteration on prompt design, then port to Java admin endpoint
- JSONL staging pattern: Python script → ai_staging/*.jsonl → POST /admin/import-sense-selections → token_sense_override → regenerate-lite-glosses
- ~5,124 single-sense lexemes need no AI (always sense 0), ~500 multi-sense lexemes (~20K-50K tokens) need per-context selection
- Redoing corpus synthesis (approach A) deferred to separate issue — not needed for fix

## Next Steps
1. Review test output from 1 Cor 1:1-5 when live results complete
2. If acceptable, run full overnight batch: `python scripts/sense_disambiguate.py --submit`
3. Build Java admin endpoint POST /admin/import-sense-selections to upsert JSONL into token_sense_override
4. Update DESIGN.md to document per-token sense selection as part of build pipeline
5. Create separate issue for approach A (corpus re-synthesis with free round-robin, dropping AGDT + NT occurrences from prompt)

## Critical Context
- InflectionEngineService.fetchAllTokens() COALESCE already supports ls_ai.gloss (from token_sense_override.sense_index) → ls.gloss (sense 0) → m.short_gloss — the infra exists, just token_sense_override is empty for 98% of tokens
- token_sense_override table: verse_word_id UNIQUE, sense_index, selected_gloss, method, confidence — JPA entity does not exist, accessed via raw JDBC
- Groq free tier: ~30 req/min for llama-3.3-70b; Gemini 2.0 Flash free: 1,500 req/day; GitHub Models free: rate-limited but available
- 1 Cor 6:1 test case (G2087): "the other of two" → should be "another" (sense 1)
- John 1:1 test case (G3056 λόγος): "word, saying, speech" → should be "Word" (divine title sense)
- Issue #140 (G2087 wrong gloss) is resolved-by #142; #139 (word sense disambiguation) closed as superseded by #142; #138 (13-word polysemy gate) superseded by #142

## Relevant Files
- scripts/sense_disambiguate.py: Full batch runner for all multi-sense tokens (Groq + Gemini round-robin, JSONL output, resume support)
- scripts/test_passage.py: Standalone test runner for single passage (--book, --chapter, --verses, --dry-run flags)
- scripts/old/run_handler_c.py: Old Handler C implementation (Claude Batch API, 13 polysemous words only) — reference for prompt and DB patterns
- ai_staging/sense_selection_YYYYMMDD_HHMMSS.jsonl: Staging output directory and file pattern
- src/main/resources/db/migration/V21__pipeline_foundation.sql: Creates token_sense_override table (already exists, not applied in current context)
- D:\workspace-vscode\interlinear-bible-api\src\main\java\com\interlinear\bible\service\InflectionEngineService.java: COALESCE query at build time joins token_sense_override → lexeme_sense
- D:\workspace-vscode\interlinear-bible-api\ai_staging/: Staging directory for JSONL output files

```python
def get_lexicon_definitions(conn, lexeme_id):
    with conn.cursor(cursor_factory=psycopg2.extras.DictCursor) as cur:
        cur.execute("""
            SELECT source, short_gloss
            FROM lexeme_meaning
            WHERE lexeme_id = %s
              AND source IN ('lsj', 'thayer', 'ugl', 'abbottsmith', 'strongs')
              AND short_gloss IS NOT NULL AND short_gloss != ''
            ORDER BY
                CASE source
                    WHEN 'ugl' THEN 1
                    WHEN 'lsj' THEN 2
                    WHEN 'thayer' THEN 3
                    WHEN 'abbottsmith' THEN 4
                    WHEN 'strongs' THEN 5
                    ELSE 6
                END
        """, (lexeme_id,))
        return [dict(r) for r in cur.fetchall()]
```

Next, add `get_verse_interlinear`:

```python
def get_verse_interlinear(conn, verse_id, target_word_id=None):
    """Fetch all Greek words in a verse with their corpus glosses."""
    with conn.cursor(cursor_factory=psycopg2.extras.DictCursor) as cur:
        cur.execute("""
            SELECT
                vw.id,
                vw.surface_form,
                vw.position,
                COALESCE(ts.selected_gloss, ls.gloss, m.short_gloss) AS our_gloss
            FROM verse_word vw
            LEFT JOIN lexeme l ON vw.lexeme_id = l.id
            LEFT JOIN lexeme_sense ls ON l.id = ls.lexeme_id AND ls.sense_index = 0
            LEFT JOIN lexeme_meaning m ON l.id = m.lexeme_id AND m.is_primary = true
            LEFT JOIN token_sense_override ts ON ts.verse_word_id = vw.id
            WHERE vw.verse_id = %s
            ORDER BY vw.position
        """, (verse_id,))
        return [dict(r) for r in cur.fetchall()]
```

Wait, this is getting complex. The `COALESCE` chain is what `InflectionEngineService` does. But for a first pass, I can simplify. Let me just get the Greek words and their current corpus sense 0 gloss.

Actually, the user's point is great - we should show what our OWN corpus says. Let me just show the Greek surface forms with the corpus sense 0 gloss as context.

```python
def get_verse_interlinear(conn, verse_id):
    with conn.cursor(cursor_factory=psycopg2.extras.DictCursor) as cur:
        cur.execute("""
            SELECT vw.id, vw.surface_form, vw.position,
                   COALESCE(ls.gloss, '?') AS corpus_gloss
            FROM verse_word vw
            LEFT JOIN lexeme l ON vw.lexeme_id = l.id
            LEFT JOIN lexeme_sense ls ON l.id = ls.lexeme_id AND ls.sense_index = 0
            WHERE vw.verse_id = %s
            ORDER BY vw.position
        """, (verse_id,))
        return [dict(r) for r in cur.fetchall()]
```

Looking at the script, for each token it:

## [assistant] (23:56:52)

I see the fix needed:

```python
def call(self, prompt):
    import time, requests
    for p in self.providers:
        for attempt in range(3):  # retry up to 3 times per provider
            elapsed = time.time() - p["last_call"]
            if elapsed < p["min_interval"]:
                time.sleep(p["min_interval"] - elapsed)
            try:
                resp = requests.post(...)
                p["last_call"] = time.time()
                if resp.status_code == 200:
                    content = resp.json()["choices"][0]["message"]["content"].strip()
                    if content.startswith("```"):
                        content = re.sub(r'```(?:json)?\s*', '', content).strip()
                    return json.loads(content), p["name"]
                elif resp.status_code == 429:
                    retry = int(resp.headers.get("Retry-After", 60))
                    wait = min(retry, 120)
                    print(f"    ⏳ {p['name']} 429 — waiting {wait}s (attempt {attempt+1}/3)")
                    time.sleep(wait)
                    continue  # retry same provider after wait
                else:
                    print(f"    ⚠ {p['name']} {resp.status_code}")
                    break
            except Exception as e:
                print(f"    ⚠ {p['name']} error: {e}")
                time.sleep(5)
                continue
    return None, None
```

Key changes:
1. Added `for attempt in range(3)` inner loop
2. On 429: wait `min(retry_after, 120)` seconds, then `continue` to retry same provider
3. On error: `continue` to retry same provider
4. Increased max sleep from 30s to 120s for rate limits

Also, I need to fix the ProviderChain.__init__ to handle the inline api_key case for GH_TOKEN:

- Handler D (InflectionEngineService) generates the LITE glosses

So Handler C runs BEFORE Handler D (gloss generation), because the correct sense needs to be selected before the inflection rules can produce the right gloss.

Our new TSR replaces Handler C. The pipeline order is:
1. Handler A: MorphKey normalization (done, Java)
2. Handler B: Sense resolution via COALESCE (done, Java — GlossChainService)

The user is pointing out that "contextual" and "sense disambiguation" seem like the same thing — both deal with picking the right meaning based on context. Let me clarify the distinction:

**ContextualGlossService** (Layer 2.5):
- Takes the *selected sense* of a word AND produces a full English gloss/phrase that reads naturally in the verse context
- Example: πίστις might have sense "faith, faithfulness, belief" — ContextualGloss might produce "the faithfulness of" or "belief in" depending on the clause context
- It generates *English renderings*, not sense selections
- It only fires for 13 specific polysemous words

**TokenSenseResolution (Handler C / TSR)**:
- Picks which pre-existing sense_index applies (0, 1, 2, etc.)
- Doesn't generate English text — just an integer + the canonical gloss for that sense
- Runs for ALL multi-sense tokens (~5,000+ lexemes), not just 13

So the pipeline is:
1. TSR picks the sense (sense_index)
2. Then ContextualGloss can take that sense and produce a better English rendering

Or more simply:
- **TSR**: "Is this πίστις sense 0 (faithfulness) or sense 1 (belief) or sense 2 (the faith/doctrine)?"
- **ContextualGloss**: "Given that we picked sense 0, the best English here is 'the faithfulness of' not just the bare word 'faith'"

They're sequential — TSR first, contextual refinement second. But the naming overlap IS confusing.

## [assistant] (10:45:22)

They *are* related, but they operate at different granularities:

**TokenSenseResolution (TSR):** Which *sense definition* applies here? Returns `sense_index` (0, 1, 2...). It's classification — picking from a menu.

**ContextualGloss:** Given that we know the sense, what's the *best English wording* for THIS specific verse? It produces a phrase, not an index. It handles supplied words, clause-level smoothing, and natural English flow.

Example with πίστις in Galatians 3:23:
- TSR picks sense 2 ("the faith/body of doctrine") because the context is about before/after Christ's coming
- ContextualGloss takes that and produces "the faith [in Christ]" with the bracketed supplied words

The pipeline is sequential: **TSR → ContextualGloss**. You need to know *which* faith before you can write the natural English for it. They're not the same layer — TSR replaces the old sense-0 fallback, ContextualGloss refines the English rendering for 13 high-value words.

But I agree the names blur. If it helps, think of them as:
- **TSR**: *Which meaning?* (classification, every token)
- **Contextual**: *How to say it in English?* (generation, 13 words only)

Clearer, or still muddy?
